Triality over Schemes Thanks: This work was supported by the NSERC grants of Mikhail Kotchetov (Discovery Grant 2018-04883) and Yorck Sommerhäuser (RGPIN-2017-06543), as well as by the AARMS collaborative research group “Groups, Rings, Lie and Hopf Algebras” and the Atlantic Algebra Centre. It was also supported by the project “Group schemes, root systems, and related representations” founded by the European Union - NextGenerationEU through Romania’s National Recovery and Resilience Plan (PNRR) call no. PNRR-III-C9-2023-I8, Project CF159/31.07.2023, and coordinated by the Ministry of Research, Innovation and Digitalization (MCID) of Romania
Cameron Ruether

Abstract




Abstract:
Working over an arbitrary base scheme, we provide an alternative development of triality which does not use Octonion algebras or symmetric composition algebras. Instead, we use the Clifford algebra of the split hyperbolic quadratic form of rank 8 and computations with Chevalley generators of groups of type
D_{4}
. Following the strategy of
[
KMRT
]
, we then define the stack of trialitarian triples and show it is equivalent to the gerbe of
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}
–torsors. We show it has endomorphisms generating a group isomorphic to
\mathbb{S}_{3}
and that several familiar cohomological properties of
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}
follow in this setting as a result. Next, we define the stack of trialitarian algebras and show it is equivalent to the gerbe of
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}\rtimes\mathbb{S}_{3}
–torsors. Because of this, it is also equivalent to the gerbes of simply connected, respectively adjoint, groups of type
D_{4}
. We define
\mathbf{Spin}_{\mathcal{T}}
and
\mathbf{PGO}^{+}_{\mathcal{T}}
for a trialitarian algebra and define concrete functors
\mathcal{T}\mapsto\mathbf{Spin}_{\mathcal{T}}
and
\mathcal{T}\mapsto\mathbf{PGO}^{+}_{\mathcal{T}}
which realize these equivalences.

Keywords: Triality,
D_{4}
, Quadratic Pairs, Chevalley Groups, Octonion Algebras, Symmetric Composition Algebras, Clifford Algebras

MSC 2020: Primary 20G35, Secondary 11E57 11E88 16H05 17A75 20G10.

Introduction

The standard development and exposition of the phenomenon of triality, that simply connected and adjoint semisimple groups of type
D_{4}
have extra outer automorphisms due to the extra symmetries of the Dynkin diagram, is built on the theory of Octonion algebras. This approach dates back to Élie Cartan starting in 1925. Later approaches, such as in
[
KMRT
]
which works over a field of characteristic not
2
, use symmetric composition algebras, which are twisted forms of para-Cayley algebras. Following
[
KMRT
]
but including characteristic
2
, Section 4 of
[
DQ21
]
utilizes para-Cayley algebras as well. The triality discussed in
[
AG19
]
over rings is also in the context of Octonions. Their definition of
\mathbf{Spin}_{8}
as triples uses Octonion multiplication explicitly and they also show how their results can be obtained by generalizing the techniques of
[
KMRT
]
. We instead present an approach which avoids the use of Octonions or symmetric composition algebras. Instead, we are able to identify and describe the phenomena of triality using the Chevalley generators of split groups of type
D_{4}
.

We work in the same setting as in
[
GNR23
]
and
[
Rue23
]
; primarily with sheaves on the fppf ringed site
(\Sch_{S},\mathcal{O})
over a base scheme
S
where
\mathcal{O}
is the global sections functor. In particular, we make no assumption on the invertibility of
2
. We write sheaves in calligraphic letters and other rings, algebras, etc., in roman letters. In order to work with groups of type
D
in this setting, we utilize quadratic pairs. See
Section

1.B
for an overview of their definition and properties. Given a central simple algebra with quadratic pair
(A,\sigma,f)
over a field (with some assumptions on the degree), in
[
DQ21
]
it was shown how to equip the associated Clifford algebra
(\mathrm{Cl}(A,\sigma,f),\underline{\sigma})
with its own canonical quadratic pair
(\underline{\sigma},\underline{f})
, where
\underline{\sigma}
is the usual canonical involution. This was extended to our setting over a scheme in
[
Rue23
]
where, for an Azumaya algebra with quadratic pair
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
over
S
, the canonical quadratic pair on the Clifford algebra
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
was defined. We similarly denote it
(\underline{\sigma},\underline{f})
. With this definition as a starting point, we are now able to describe triality over schemes as well.

Our work here starts with the isomorphism of algebras with quadratic pair
[
Rue23
, (4)]
(which is built on the isomorphisms of algebras from
[
CF15
]
and
[
Knu91
]
). Let
(\mathcal{O}^{8},q)
be the split hyperbolic quadratic form of rank
8
, in detail,
q(x_{1},\ldots,x_{8})=x_{1}x_{8}+x_{2}x_{7}+x_{3}x_{6}+x_{4}x_{5}
. Let
(\Mat_{8}(\mathcal{O}),\sigma,f)
be its adjoint quadratic triple. After choosing appropriate bases, the isomorphism in
[
Rue23
]
can be written as

\Phi\colon\big(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell\big(\Mat_{8}(\mathcal{O}),\sigma,f\big),\underline{\sigma},\underline{f}\big)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(\Mat_{8}(\mathcal{O}),\sigma,f)\times(\Mat_{8}(\mathcal{O}),\sigma,f).

By analyzing the images of the Chevalley generators of
\mathbf{Spin}_{8}\subset\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\Mat_{8}(\mathcal{O}),\sigma,f)
under
\Phi
, we show how one can identify the phenomenon of triality within the isomorphism
\Phi
. Precisely, we show the following.

Theorem A
.

The isomorphism
\Phi
restricts to a group homomorphism
\mathbf{Spin}_{8}\to\mathbf{O}_{8}^{+}\times\mathbf{O}_{8}^{+}
which induces the homomorphism

\displaystyle\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}\times\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\theta^{+}(x),(\theta^{+})^{2}(x)\big)

where
\theta^{+}
is an order three outer automorphism of
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}
.

Readers familiar with triality will know that the usual descriptions of the phenomena come from a similar looking isomorphism of algebras

\big(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{S}),\sigma_{n}),\underline{\sigma_{n}}\big)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{S}),\sigma_{n})\times(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{S}),\sigma_{n})

where
(\mathcal{S},n)
is a symmetric composition algebra with its quadratic norm. Here,
\sigma_{n}
is the adjoint involution of
n
. The isomorphism above is defined using properties of the non-associative multiplication in
\mathcal{S}
. We compare our Chevalley generator based approach to this usual approach in
Section

2.C
, showing, as one would hope, that they are equivalent.

Once we have the result of
Theorem

A
, we no longer need Chevalley generators or symmetric composition algebras and we proceed down a path analogous to
[
KMRT
]
. To shorten notation, we write
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{qt}}=(\mathcal{A},\sigma_{\mathcal{A}},f_{\mathcal{A}})
for an Azumaya algebra with quadratic pair (qt standing for
quadratic triple
, the term used in
[
GNR23
]
and
[
Rue23
]
). We begin by defining trialitarian triples in the same way as in
[
KMRT
]
,
[
DQ21
]
, or
[
BT23
]
, i.e., they are the data of
(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{qt}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{qt}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{qt}},\alpha_{\mathcal{A}})
, where
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{qt}}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{qt}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{qt}}
are quadratic triples of degree
8
and

\alpha_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\big(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{qt}}),\underline{\sigma_{\mathcal{A}}},\underline{f_{\mathcal{A}}}\big)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{qt}}\times\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{qt}}

is an isomorphism of quadratic triples. By considering quadratic triples over differing base schemes, these form a stack over
\Sch_{S}
which we denote by
\mathfrak{Trip}
.

Theorem B
.

The stack
\mathfrak{Trip}
is equivalent to the gerbe of
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}
–torsors. Furthermore, there are stack endomorphisms

\displaystyle\Theta^{+}\colon\mathfrak{Trip}

\displaystyle\to\mathfrak{Trip}

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{qt}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{qt}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{qt}},\alpha_{\mathcal{A}})

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{qt}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{qt}},\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{qt}},\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Theta\colon\mathfrak{Trip}

\displaystyle\to\mathfrak{Trip}

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{qt}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{qt}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{qt}},\alpha_{\mathcal{A}})

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{qt}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{qt}},\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{qt}},\mathrm{sw}\circ\alpha_{\mathcal{A}})

where
\mathrm{sw}\colon\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{qt}}\times\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{qt}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{qt}}\times\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{qt}}
is the switch isomorphism. These satisfy the relations

(\Theta^{+})^{3}=\Id,\quad\Theta^{2}=\Id,\text{ and }\Theta^{+}\circ\Theta=\Theta\circ(\Theta^{+})^{2}.

The fact that
\mathfrak{Trip}
is equivalent to the gerbe of
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}
–torsors follows from showing that the automorphism sheaf of a designated split object in
\mathfrak{Trip}
is
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}
. The morphisms
\Theta^{+}
and
\Theta
are stack level analogues of outer automorphisms
\theta^{+}
and
\theta
of
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}
which generate
\Out(\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+})(T)\cong\mathbb{S}_{3}(T)
over a connected scheme
T
. The existence of these endomorphisms recovers the results of
[
KMRT
, 42.3]
or
[
DQ21
, 4.11]
in our setting, that if we have Azumaya algebras of degree
8
with quadratic pairs such that
\big(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{qt}}),\underline{\sigma_{\mathcal{A}}},\underline{f_{\mathcal{A}}}\big)\cong\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{qt}}\times\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{qt}}
, then also

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{qt}}),\underline{\sigma_{\mathcal{B}}},\underline{f_{\mathcal{B}}}\big)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{qt}}\times\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{qt}},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{qt}}),\underline{\sigma_{\mathcal{C}}},\underline{f_{\mathcal{C}}}\big)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{qt}}\times\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{qt}}.

Because we have worked on the level of stacks, we obtain as an easy corollary the description of how the outer automorphisms
\theta^{+}
and
\theta
act on the first cohomology set
H^{1}(S,\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+})
, which classifies trialitarian triples up to isomorphism.

The final goal is to mirror the classical correspondence between algebraic groups and algebras with involution for the case of type
D_{4}
. This is done over fields in
[
KMRT
, §43]
by defining trialitarian algebras. In
Section

4
, we define trialitarian algebras over a scheme and assemble them into a gerbe,
\mathfrak{TriAlg}
, whose split object has automorphism sheaf
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}\rtimes\mathbb{S}_{3}
. This means
\mathfrak{TriAlg}
is equivalent to the gerbe of
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}\rtimes\mathbb{S}_{3}
–torsors. Therefore, it will abstractly be equivalent to the gerbe of simply connected groups of type
D_{4}
as well as the gerbe of adjoint groups of type
D_{4}
. In
Section

5
, for a trialitarian algebra
\mathcal{T}
, we define the groups
\mathbf{PGO}^{+}_{\mathcal{T}}
and
\mathbf{Spin}_{\mathcal{T}}
and show that these definitions provide concrete equivalences of gerbes.

Theorem C
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{sc}}
and
\mathfrak{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{ad}}
be the gerbes of simply connected, respectively adjoint, groups of type
D_{4}
over
S
. There are equivalences of gerbes

\displaystyle\mathfrak{TriAlg}

\displaystyle\to\mathfrak{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{sc}}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{TriAlg}

\displaystyle\to\mathfrak{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{ad}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{Spin}_{\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{PGO}^{+}_{\mathcal{T}}.

Furthermore, there are canonical surjections
\mathbf{Spin}_{\mathcal{T}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{PGO}^{+}_{\mathcal{T}}
, whose kernels are the respective centers, which induce an equivalence of categories
\mathfrak{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{sc}}\to\mathfrak{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{ad}}
which makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{sc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{TriAlg}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{D}_{4}^{\mathrm{ad}}}

commute.

This paper is organized as follows. In the preliminaries, we establish our setting over a scheme and review the necessary definitions and background results for key objects such as quadratic triples, the groups
\mathbf{Spin}(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
,
\mathbf{O}^{+}(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
, and
\mathbf{PGO}^{+}(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
, as well as stacks, cohomology, and étale covers. Our choices of Chevalley generators for the split groups
\mathbf{Spin}_{2n}
,
\mathbf{O}^{+}_{2n}
, and
\mathbf{PGO}^{+}_{2n}
are described in
Section

1.C.ii
. Moving into
Section

2.C
, we begin by reviewing the automorphism groups of our split groups in terms of Chevalley generators.
Theorem

A
is justified in
Section

2.B
where the necessary computations are the subject of
Lemma

2.3
. As mentioned above,
Section

2.C
compares our approach to the usual methods of describing triality.

Section

3
is where we deal with all things trialitarian triple and justify
Theorem

B
. The fact that
\mathfrak{Trip}
is equivalent to the gerbe of
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}
–torsors follows from
Lemma

3.3
(ii)
where we show that the automorphism sheaf of the designated split object in
\mathfrak{Trip}
is
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}
. The morphisms
\Theta^{+}
and
\Theta
are constructed explicitly over each fiber throughout
Section

3.B
and the relations between them are shown in
Proposition

3.12
.

Section

4
is about trialitarian algebras. In order to define them, we use what we call the trialitarian cover
\Sigma_{2}(L)
of a degree
3
étale cover
L\to S
as well as various morphisms involving
\Sigma_{2}(L)
, all reviewed in the preliminaries. Using these morphisms, we define trialitarian algebras and the stack
\mathfrak{TriAlg}
. We show in
Lemma

4.11
that
\mathfrak{TriAlg}
is equivalent to the gerbe of
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}\rtimes\mathbb{S}_{3}
–torsors.

Finally, we discuss the simply connected and adjoint groups of type
D_{4}
in
Section

5
. Their definitions are given in
Definition

5.4
and
Definition

5.1
respectively.
Theorem

C
is justified across
Theorem

5.3
,
Theorem

5.6
, and
Remark

5.7
.

1.
Preliminaries

1.A.
Setting over a Scheme

We work in the same setting as in
[
Rue23
]
, which we review here. We fix a base scheme
S
and primarily work with sheaves over the big fppf ringed site
(\Sch_{S},\mathcal{O})
where
\Sch_{S}
is as in
[
Stacks
, Tag 021R]
and
\mathcal{O}
is the global sections sheaf of
[
Stacks
, Tag 03DU]
. Covers in the fppf topology will be denoted by
\{T_{i}\to T\}_{i\in I}
with
T_{ij}=T_{i}\times_{T}T_{j}
. We denote by
\Aff_{S}
the big affine site over
S
as in
[
Stacks
, Tag 021S (2)]
. Affine schemes will usually be denoted by
U
or
V
. We let

(i)

\Sets
be the category of sets,

(ii)

\Grp
be the category of groups,

(iii)

\Ab
be the category of abelian groups, and

(iv)

\Rings
be the category of unital commutative associative rings.

We use restriction notation as in
[
Stacks
]
. In particular, if
\mathcal{F}
is a sheaf on
\Sch_{S}
and
T\in\Sch_{S}
, we denote by
\mathcal{F}|_{T}
the sheaf restricted to the subcategory
\Sch_{T}
. If we have a morphism
T^{\prime}\to T
and a section
x\in\mathcal{F}(T)
, then
x|_{T^{\prime}}
denotes the image of
x
under the restriction map
\mathcal{F}(T)\to\mathcal{F}(T^{\prime})
. A phrase like “for appropriate sections
x\in\mathcal{F}
and
y\in\mathcal{G}
” means that we consider all
T\in\Sch_{S}
and pairs
x\in\mathcal{F}(T)
and
y\in\mathcal{G}(T)
.

Modules and algebras will be over the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}
. We use
\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{o\hskip-0.86108ptm}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2})
to denote the sheaf of internal homomorphisms between two
\mathcal{O}
–modules. We set
\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{o\hskip-0.86108ptm}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M})
and set
\mathbf{Aut}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M})
to be the subsheaf of automorphisms. For an
\mathcal{O}
–module
\mathcal{M}
, its
dual
is
\mathcal{M}^{*}=\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{o\hskip-0.86108ptm}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{O})
. Modules can be finite locally free, everywhere positive rank, and/or of constant rank (among other properties) as in
[
Stacks
, Tag 03DL (2)]
. By
[
Stacks
, Tag 03DN]
, it is sufficient to check these conditions over a cover of
S
.

We make extensive use of Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebras, referring to
[
For17
]
for the background on Azumaya algebras over rings. We use the definition
[
CF15
, 2.5.3.4]
, stating that
\mathcal{A}
is an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebra if

(i)

For each affine scheme
U\in\Aff_{S}
,
\mathcal{A}(U)
is an Azumaya algebra over the ring
\mathcal{O}(U)
.

This is equivalent to each of the following two conditions.

(ii)

There exists a cover
\{T_{i}\to S\}_{i\in I}
such that for each
i\in I
,
\mathcal{A}|_{T_{i}}\cong\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{T_{i}}}(\mathcal{M}_{i})
for some
\mathcal{O}|_{T_{i}}
–module
\mathcal{M}_{i}
which is finite locally free of everywhere positive rank.

(iii)

There exists a cover
\{T_{i}\to S\}_{i\in I}
such that for each
i\in I
,
\mathcal{A}|_{T_{i}}\cong\Mat_{n_{i}}(\mathcal{O}|_{T_{i}})
for some
n_{i}>0
.

We use the
reduced norm

\Nrd_{\mathcal{B}}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{O}
and
reduced trace

\Trd_{\mathcal{B}}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{O}
of a separable algebra
\mathcal{B}
. When case the algebra is Azumaya, these are descended versions of the familiar determinant and trace of matrix algebras, respectively. For an Azumaya algebra
\mathcal{A}
, we set
\mathfrak{sl}_{\mathcal{A}}=\Ker(\Trd_{\mathcal{A}})
to be the submodule of trace zero sections. In case
\mathcal{B}
is not Azumaya, but is instead locally a cartesian product of finitely many Azumaya algebras, then
\Trd_{\mathcal{B}}
is locally the sum of the traces. Precisely, if
\{T_{i}\to S\}_{i\in I}
is a cover such that

\mathcal{B}|_{T_{i}}\cong\mathcal{A}_{i,1}\times\ldots\times\mathcal{A}_{i,n}

for Azumaya
\mathcal{O}|_{T_{i}}
–algebras
\mathcal{A}_{i,1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{i,n}
, then

\Trd_{\mathcal{B}}|_{T_{i}}=\Trd_{\mathcal{A}_{i,1}}+\ldots+\Trd_{\mathcal{A}_{i,n}}\colon\mathcal{A}_{i,1}\times\ldots\times\mathcal{A}_{i,n}\to\mathcal{O}|_{T_{i}}.

1.B.
Quadratic Forms, Quadratic Triples, and Clifford Algebras

We refer to
[
Knu91
]
for background on quadratic forms over rings. For further background on quadratic triples over schemes we refer to
[
CF15
]
and
[
GNR23
]
.

Given a quadratic form
q\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{O}
on an
\mathcal{O}
–module
\mathcal{M}
, we call
(\mathcal{M},q)
a
quadratic module
. Its polar bilinear form is denoted by
b_{q}\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{O}
. A general bilinear form
b\colon\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{O}
is called
regular
if the map

\displaystyle b^{*}\colon\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{M}^{*}

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle\mapsto b(m,\underline{\hskip 8.61108pt})

is an isomorphism. A quadratic form
q
or quadratic module
(\mathcal{M},q)
is called
regular
if
b_{q}
is regular. Every regular bilinear form has an associated adjoint involution
\sigma_{b}
on the algebra
\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M})
. A regular bilinear form also induces an isomorphism

(1)

\displaystyle\varphi_{b}\colon\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle m_{1}\otimes m_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto b(m_{1},\underline{\hskip 8.61108pt})\cdot m_{2}.

By an involution
\sigma
on an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebra
\mathcal{A}
, we mean an
\mathcal{O}
–linear anti-automorphism. Over a sufficiently fine cover, any such involution
\sigma
will be the adjoint involution of some regular bilinear forms. We call
\sigma

(i)

orthogonal
if the bilinear forms are symmetric,

(ii)

weakly-symplectic
if the bilinear forms are skew-symmetric, and

(iii)

symplectic
if the bilinear forms are alternating.

Every symplectic involution is weakly-symplectic. If
\frac{1}{2}\in\mathcal{O}
, then weakly-symplectic and symplectic coincide and they are disjoint from orthogonal. However, in the case when
2=0\in\mathcal{O}
, the notions of orthogonal and weakly-symplectic coincide. If
\mathcal{B}
is a separable
\mathcal{O}
–algebra, then an involution
\sigma
on
\mathcal{B}
will be called orthogonal if it acts trivially on the center and locally whenever
\mathcal{B}
factors as a product of Azumaya algebras, the restriction of
\sigma
to each Azumaya factor is orthogonal. We call
\sigma
weakly-symplectic or symplectic similarly.

Given a quadratic form
q\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{O}
, using the definition over a scheme from
[
CF15
, 4.2]
, we have the
Clifford algebra

\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{M},q)=\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{M})/\mathcal{I}

where
\mathcal{I}
is the subsheaf of two-sided ideals generated by elements of the form
m\otimes m-q(m)
. It has a canonical involution
\underline{\sigma}
which reverses the tensor factors and hence is the identity on the submodule isomorphic to
\mathcal{M}
. By
[
CF15
, 4.2.0.7]
, if
U\in\Aff_{S}
, then
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{M},q)(U)\cong\mathrm{Cl}(\mathcal{M}(U),q(U))
where the right hand side is the Clifford algebra over a ring as in
[
Knu91
, IV.1.1.2]
. As usual, we denote the even Clifford algebra generated by products of even length by
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathcal{M},q)
. The canonical involution restricts to
\underline{\sigma}_{0}
on
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathcal{M},q)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an
\mathcal{O}
–module and
\sigma\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
an order two endomorphism. We have two maps
\Id\pm\sigma\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
and we define the submodules

\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{M},\sigma}=\Ker(\Id-\sigma)\text{, }\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{k\hskip-0.86108pte\hskip-1.29167ptw}_{\mathcal{M},\sigma}=\Ker(\Id+\sigma)\text{, and }\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{M},\sigma}=\Img(\Id-\sigma)

where
\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{M},\sigma}
is an image sheaf and therefore involves sheafification.

A
quadratic triple
is
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
where
(\mathcal{A},\sigma)
is an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebra with orthogonal involution and
f\colon\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}\to\mathcal{O}
is an
\mathcal{O}
–linear map satisfying

f(a+\sigma(a))=\Trd_{\mathcal{A}}(a)

for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
. We call the function
f
a
semi-trace
and note that
(\sigma,f)
is commonly referred to as a
quadratic pair
on
\mathcal{A}
. A morphism of quadratic triples
\varphi\colon(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)\to(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},f^{\prime})
is a morphism of the Azumaya algebras satisfying
\varphi\circ\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}\circ\varphi
and
f^{\prime}\circ\varphi=f
.

If
(\mathcal{M},q)
is a regular quadratic module, then by
[
GNR23
, 4.4]
it corresponds to a unique quadratic triple
(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M}),\sigma_{q},f_{q})
where
\sigma_{q}
is the adjoint involution of
b_{q}
and the semi-trace satisfies

f_{q}(\varphi_{b}(m\otimes m))=q(m)

for all
m\in\mathcal{M}
. The triple
(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M}),\sigma_{q},f_{q})
is called the
adjoint
of
(\mathcal{M},q)
.

By
[
GNR23
, 3.6(iii)]
, if
\sigma
is an orthogonal involution, then the submodules
\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}
and
\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}
are mutually orthogonal with respect to the trace form on
\mathcal{A}
. Therefore, any element
\lambda\in\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}(S)
gives a well defined map
g_{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}\to\mathcal{O}
which is defined locally over those
T\in\Sch_{S}
for which there exists
\ell_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{A}(T)
with
\ell_{\lambda}\mapsto\lambda|_{T}
by
\Trd_{\mathcal{A}}(\ell_{\lambda}\underline{\hskip 8.61108pt})
. Not all such
g_{\lambda}
are semi-traces, but by
[
GNR23
, 4.18]
, given a quadratic triple
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
there exists a unique element
\lambda_{f}\in\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{A},\sigma}(S)
such that
f=g_{\lambda_{f}}
.

Let
(\mathcal{B},\sigma)
be a separable
\mathcal{O}
–algebra with orthogonal involution. A linear map
f\colon\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{B},\sigma}\to\mathcal{O}
satisfying
f(b+\sigma(b))=\Trd_{\mathcal{B}}(b)
for all
b\in\mathcal{B}
will also be called a
semi-trace
. The triple
(\mathcal{B},\sigma,f)
will be called an
algebra with semi-trace
to distinguish it from a quadratic triple, which assumes that
\mathcal{B}
is Azumaya. An algebra with semi-trace will locally be a cartesian product of quadratic triples. The process of defining a semi-trace using an element
\lambda\in\mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{B},\sigma}
works analogously by working sufficiently locally. We note that our terminology differs slightly from
[
DQ21
]
where
(\sigma,f)
would also be called a quadratic pair on
\mathcal{B}
.

Associated to a quadratic triple
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
is a Clifford algebra
(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f),\underline{\sigma})
with canonical involution defined in
[
CF15
, 4.2.0.13]
or equivalently in
[
Rue23
, 1.3]
. It comes with a canonical linear map
c\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
whose image generates
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
as an algebra. The canonical involution is defined by
\underline{\sigma}(c(a))=c(\sigma(a))
. By
[
CF15
, 4.2.0.14]
, if
(\mathcal{M},q)
is a regular quadratic module of constant even rank, then there is an isomorphism of algebras with involution

(2)

\Phi\colon(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathcal{M},q),\underline{\sigma}_{0})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M}),\sigma_{q},f_{q}),\underline{\sigma_{q}})

induced by the isomorphism of modules
\varphi_{b}\colon\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{M}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M})
of
Equation

1
. If
\mathcal{A}
is of degree
2n
, then by
[
Rue23
, 1.9]
,
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
is locally a Cartesian product of two Azumaya algebras and the involution
\underline{\sigma}

(i)

does not act trivially on the center if
n
is odd,

(ii)

is orthogonal if
n\equiv 0\pmod{4}
, and

(iii)

is symplectic if
n\equiv 2\pmod{4}
.

In particular, due to our conventions,
\underline{\sigma}
is an orthogonal involution if and only if
n\equiv 0\pmod{4}
or both
n\equiv 2\pmod{4}
and
2=0\in\mathcal{O}
. In the cases where
\deg(\mathcal{A})\geq 8
and
\underline{\sigma}
is orthogonal, then the Clifford algebra also has a canonical semi-trace
\underline{f}
defined in
[
Rue23
, 2.7]
. There is a commutative diagram with exact rows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{sl}_{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.90417pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f),\underline{\sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.90417pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f),\underline{\sigma}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Trd_{\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

and the canonical semi-trace
\underline{f}
is defined to be the linear map
g_{\rho(1)}\colon\mathcal{S}\hskip-2.15277pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptm}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.90417pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f),\underline{\sigma}}\to\mathcal{O}
associated to
\rho(1)\in(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)/\mathcal{A}\hskip-0.43057pt\textit{$\ell$\hskip-1.29167ptt}_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.90417pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f),\underline{\sigma}})(S)
. By
[
Rue23
, 2.12]
, this construction extends the usual functoriality of the Clifford algebra with respect to isomorphisms. Given an isomorphism of quadratic triples
\varphi\colon(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)\to(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},f^{\prime})
, there is an associated isomorphism of Clifford algebras with semi-trace

(3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\varphi)\colon(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f),\underline{\sigma},\underline{f})

\displaystyle\to(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},f^{\prime}),\underline{\sigma^{\prime}},\underline{f^{\prime}})

\displaystyle c(a)

\displaystyle\mapsto c(\varphi(a)).

We will frequently use the Clifford algebra of a hyperbolic quadratic form for calculations. We recall from
[
Rue23
, 1.5]
the relevant details of such a Clifford algebra and fix our conventions.

Example 1.1
.

We let
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{O}^{n}
be the free
\mathcal{O}
–module of rank
n
, and set
\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V})=\mathcal{V}\oplus\mathcal{V}^{*}
. Let
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
be the standard basis of
\mathcal{V}
and
\{v_{n}^{*},\ldots,v_{1}^{*}\}
the dual basis of
\mathcal{V}^{*}
. We define a hyperbolic quadratic form on
\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V})
by setting
q_{2n}(x+g)=g(x)
for
x\in\mathcal{V}
and
g\in\mathcal{V}^{*}
. This is a regular quadratic form. The Clifford algebra
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),q_{2n})
is generated by the elements
v_{i}
and
v_{j}^{*}
subject to the relations

\displaystyle v_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle(v_{i}^{*})^{2}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle v_{i}v_{j}

\displaystyle=-v_{j}v_{i}

\displaystyle v_{i}^{*}v_{j}^{*}

\displaystyle=-v_{j}^{*}v_{i}^{*}

\displaystyle v_{i}v_{i}^{*}

\displaystyle=1-v_{i}^{*}v_{i}

\displaystyle v_{i}v_{j}^{*}

\displaystyle=-v_{j}^{*}v_{i}

for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
with
i\neq j
.

Let
\wedge\mathcal{V}
be the exterior algebra of
\mathcal{V}
and let
\wedge_{0}\mathcal{V}
and
\wedge_{1}\mathcal{V}
be the even and odd parts respectively. We define two families of endomorphisms of
\wedge\mathcal{V}
. For
x\in\mathcal{V}
, let
\ell_{x}\colon\wedge\mathcal{V}\to\wedge\mathcal{V}
be the left multiplication by
x
. For
g\in\mathcal{V}^{*}
, define
d_{g}\colon\wedge\mathcal{V}\to\wedge\mathcal{V}
by

d_{g}(x_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge x_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{i+1}g(x_{i})\cdot x_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\hat{x_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge x_{k}

where hat denotes omission. Then by
[
CF15
, 4.2.0.10]
(which follows from
[
Knu91
, IV.2.1.1]
), there is an isomorphism of algebras

\displaystyle\Phi\colon\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),q_{2n})

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\wedge\mathcal{V})

\displaystyle v_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\ell_{v_{i}}

\displaystyle v_{i}^{*}

\displaystyle\mapsto d_{v_{i}^{*}}

which restricts to an isomorphism

\Phi_{0}\colon\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),q_{2n})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\wedge_{0}\mathcal{V})\times\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\wedge_{1}\mathcal{V}).

The
\mathcal{O}
–module
\wedge\mathcal{V}
is equipped with a bilinear form following
[
KMRT
, pg.90]
. Let
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
be the set of integers from
1
to
n
, and then for
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\subseteq[n]
written in increasing order, we set
v_{I}=v_{i_{1}}\wedge v_{i_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge v_{i_{k}}
. These
v_{I}
form a basis of
\wedge\mathcal{V}
. Now, for
x\in\wedge\mathcal{V}
let
\overline{x}
be the element with the wedge factors reversed, i.e.,
\overline{x_{1}\wedge x_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge x_{k}}=x_{k}\wedge\ldots\wedge x_{2}\wedge x_{1}
and extended linearly. Let
\pi\colon\wedge\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{O}
be the map coming from the projection onto the submodule spanned by
e_{[n]}
. Define a bilinear form
b_{\wedge}\colon\wedge\mathcal{V}\times\wedge\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{O}
by

b_{\wedge}(x,y)=\pi(\overline{x}\wedge y).

By
[
Rue23
, 1.7]
,
b_{\wedge}
is a regular bilinear form whose adjoint involution
\sigma_{\wedge}
corresponds to
\underline{\sigma}
under the isomorphism
\Phi
. When
n\equiv 0,1\pmod{4}
or
2=0\in\mathcal{O}
then
b_{\wedge}
is the polar of a quadratic form
q_{\wedge}
defined in
[
Rue23
, Eq (6)]
.

Let
(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),\sigma_{2n},f_{2n})
be the adjoint quadratic triple of
(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),q_{2n})
. We identify
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),q_{2n})=\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),\sigma_{2n},f_{2n})
. When
n\geq 4
and
\underline{\sigma_{2n}}_{0}
is orthogonal, i.e. when
n\equiv 0\pmod{4}
or both
n\equiv 2\pmod{4}
and
2=0\in\mathcal{O}
, we have restricted bilinear forms
b_{\wedge i}
and quadratic forms
q_{\wedge i}
on each
\wedge_{i}\mathcal{V}
, as well as restricted orthogonal involutions
\sigma_{\wedge i}
on each
\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\wedge_{i}\mathcal{V})
. By
[
Rue23
, 2.11]
, the isomorphism
\Phi_{0}
then becomes an isomorphism of Clifford algebras with canonical semi-trace

\textnormal{(4) }\Phi_{0}\colon\!(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),q_{2n}),\underline{\sigma_{2n}}_{0},\underline{f_{2n}})\!\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\!(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\wedge_{0}\mathcal{V}),\sigma_{\wedge 0},f_{\wedge 0})\!\times\!(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\wedge_{1}\mathcal{V}),\sigma_{\wedge 1},f_{\wedge 1}).

1.C.
Algebraic Groups

We are interested in linear algebraic groups of type
D
, which we consider as sheaves of groups on
\Sch_{S}
. For the split groups arising from a hyperbolic quadratic form, we will also work with their subpresheaf of elementary groups generated by their Chevalley generators.

1.C.i.
Relevant Definitions

We recall the definitions of some key groups from
[
CF15
]
and fix our notation.

If
\mathcal{B}
is an
\mathcal{O}
–algebra, the
general linear group
of
\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle\mathbf{GL}_{\mathcal{B}}\colon\Sch_{S}

\displaystyle\to\Grp

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{B}(T)^{\times}.

is the subsheaf of invertible elements in
\mathcal{B}
. The
projective general linear group
is the group of algebra automorphisms
\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathbf{Aut}_{\mathcal{O}\mathrm{\mathchar 45\relax alg}}(\mathcal{B})
. If
\mathcal{M}
is an
\mathcal{O}
–module, we set
\mathbf{GL}_{\mathcal{M}}=\mathbf{GL}_{\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M})}=\mathbf{Aut}_{\mathcal{O}\mathrm{\mathchar 45\relax mod}}(\mathcal{M})
and
\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{M}}=\mathbf{PGL}_{\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M})}
. We set
\mathbf{GL}_{n}=\mathbf{GL}_{\mathcal{O}^{n}}\cong\mathbf{GL}_{\Mat_{n}(\mathcal{O})}
and
\mathbf{PGL}_{n}
similarly.

The group of
n^{\textnormal{th}}
–roots of unity is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}\colon\Sch_{S}

\displaystyle\to\Grp

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\{x\in\mathcal{O}(T)\mid x^{n}=1\}.

Let
(\mathcal{M},q)
be a regular quadratic module. The
orthogonal group
is the group of automorphisms which fix
q
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{O}_{q}\colon\Sch_{S}

\displaystyle\to\Grp

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\{\varphi\in\mathbf{GL}_{\mathcal{M}}(T)\mid q|_{T}\circ\varphi=q|_{T}\}.

When
(\mathcal{M},q)=(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),q_{2n})
is the even rank hyperbolic quadratic form of
Example

1.1
, we write
\mathbf{O}_{q_{2n}}=\mathbf{O}_{2n}
. The
spin group
of
(\mathcal{M},q)
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{Spin}_{q}\colon\Sch_{S}

\displaystyle\to\Grp

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\{a\in\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathcal{M},q)(T)\mid a\underline{\sigma}(a)=1,\;\Inn(a)(\mathcal{M}|_{T})=\mathcal{M}|_{T}\}

where
\Inn(a)
is the inner automorphism of
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{M},q)|_{T}
defined by the section
a\in\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathcal{M},q)(T)
, and as usual we view
\mathcal{M}|_{T}
as a submodule of
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{M},q)|_{T}
via the canonical inclusion. For
(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),q_{2n})
of
Example

1.1
, we write
\mathbf{Spin}_{2n}
. The spin group comes with a surjective map of sheaves

\displaystyle\chi\colon\mathbf{Spin}_{q}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{O}_{q}

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto\Inn(a)|_{\mathcal{M}},

called the
vector representation
, whose kernel is isomorphic to
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
be a quadratic triple. The
orthogonal group
of
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{O}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}\colon\Sch_{S}

\displaystyle\to\Grp

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\{a\in\mathcal{A}(T)\mid a\sigma(a)=1,\;f|_{T}\circ\Inn(a)=f|_{T}\}

where
\Inn(a)\colon\mathcal{A}|_{T}\to\mathcal{A}|_{T}
is the inner automorphism defined by the section
a\in\mathcal{A}(T)
. By
[
CF15
, 4.4.0.44]
, for a regular quadratic module
(\mathcal{M},q)
there is a canonical isomorphism
\mathbf{O}_{q}\cong\mathbf{O}_{(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M}),\sigma_{q},f_{q})}
. The
projective orthogonal group
is the group of quadratic triple automorphisms

\mathbf{PGO}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}=\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f).

We set
\mathbf{PGO}_{q}=\mathbf{PGO}_{(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M}),\sigma_{q},f_{q})}
. For
(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),q_{2n})
of
Example

1.1
, we write
\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}
. The canonical projection

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbf{O}}\colon\mathbf{O}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{PGO}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto\Inn(a)

is a surjective map of sheaves with kernel isomorphic to
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
.

The spin group of a quadratic triple is defined over a field in
[
KMRT
]
using the Clifford bimodule and the generalization of the same approach is used in
[
CF15
]
, though the authors omit the technicalities. By
[
CF15
, 4.5.2.3]
, combined with their definition
[
CF15
, 4.5.2.5]
, they show that for the adjoint quadratic triple of a regular quadratic form
(\mathcal{M},q)
, there is an isomorphism

\mathbf{Spin}_{q}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{Spin}_{(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M}),\sigma_{q},f_{q})}

which is the restriction of the isomorphism
\Phi
of
Equation

2
. This allows us to also avoid the technicalities of the Clifford bimodule by using the following descent flavoured definition.

Lemma 1.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
be a quadratic triple. For each
T\in\Sch_{S}
, let
\mathcal{U}_{T}
be the class of
T
–schemes over which
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
becomes adjoint to a regular quadratic form, i.e., for all
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{T}
we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)|_{T^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\cong(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{T^{\prime}}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}),\sigma_{q^{\prime}},f_{q^{\prime}}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)|_{T^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},q^{\prime})

for a regular quadratic
\mathcal{O}|_{T^{\prime}}
–module
(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},q^{\prime})
. Then, the spin group of
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
can be described as

\displaystyle\mathbf{Spin}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}\colon\Sch_{S}

\displaystyle\to\Grp

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\{a\in\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)\mid\Phi_{T^{\prime}}^{-1}(a|_{T^{\prime}})\in\mathbf{Spin}_{q^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})\text{ for all }T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{T}\}.

where
\Phi_{T^{\prime}}
is the isomorphism of
Equation

2
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the sheaf

T\mapsto\{a\in\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)\mid\Phi_{T^{\prime}}^{-1}(a|_{T^{\prime}})\in\mathbf{Spin}_{q^{\prime}}(T^{\prime})\text{ for all }T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{T}\}.

For a scheme
T\in\Sch_{S}
, if
T\in\mathcal{U}_{T}
, i.e.,
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)|_{T}
is adjoint to a regular quadratic
\mathcal{O}|_{T}
–module
(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},q^{\prime})
, then it is clear that
\Phi_{T}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}(T))=\mathbf{Spin}_{q^{\prime}}(T)
, or equivalently,
\mathcal{F}(T)=\mathbf{Spin}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}(T)
. Since there exists an fppf cover
\{T_{i}\to S\}_{i\in I}
such that
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)|_{T_{i}}
is adjoint to a regular quadratic module for each
i\in I
, and since
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathbf{Spin}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}
share the same restriction maps because they are both subsheaves of
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
, this means that
\mathcal{F}=\mathbf{Spin}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}
as claimed.
∎

These spin groups also come with a homomorphism
\chi\colon\mathbf{Spin}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}\to\mathbf{O}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}
which is locally the vector representation defined above.

Let
(\mathcal{M},q)
be a regular quadratic module. We have group homomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{\Inn}\colon\mathbf{Spin}_{q}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{M},q),\underline{\sigma}),

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto\Inn(a)

(5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}\colon\mathbf{O}_{q}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{M},q),\underline{\sigma}),

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(m\mapsto B(m)\big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell\colon\mathbf{PGO}_{q}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathcal{M},q),\underline{\sigma}_{0})

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(m_{1}\otimes m_{2}\mapsto(\varphi_{b_{q}}^{-1}\circ\psi\circ\varphi_{b_{q}})(m_{1}\otimes m_{2})\big),

where
\mathbf{C}(B)
and
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\psi)
are defined uniquely by their action on generators given above, where
\varphi_{b_{q}}
is the isomorphism of
Equation

1
. Of course, for
\psi\in\mathbf{PGO}_{q}
, its image
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\psi)
is defined by the functoriality of the Clifford algebra after accounting for the isomorphism
\Phi
of
Equation

2
, which is itself induced by
\varphi_{b_{q}}
. Note that the images of both
\mathbf{C}_{\Inn}
and
\mathbf{C}
also stabilize the even Clifford algebra, and so by abuse of notation we will also discuss homomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{\Inn}\colon\mathbf{Spin}_{q}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathcal{M},q),\underline{\sigma}_{0}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}\colon\mathbf{O}_{q}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathcal{M},q),\underline{\sigma}_{0}).

We then get commutative diagrams

(6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Spin}_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{O}_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathcal{M},q),\underline{\sigma}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PGO}_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}_{\Inn}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbf{O}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.90417pt\ell}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Spin}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{O}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f),\underline{\sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PGO}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}_{\Inn}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbf{O}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.90417pt\ell}

where in the left diagram, by definition of
\chi\colon\mathbf{Spin}_{q}\to\mathbf{O}_{q}
, we have that
\mathbf{C}_{\Inn}=\mathbf{C}\circ\chi
, so the top triangle commutes and the bottom triangle commutes by
[
Rue23
, 1.14]
. We reuse notation for the diagram on the right since it is the twisted version of the diagram on the left, i.e., both diagrams agree locally. On the right,
\mathbf{C}_{\Inn}
still sends an element to its inner automorphism and now
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell
is directly given by the functoriality of the Clifford algebra. In the cases when the Clifford algebra has its canonical quadratic triple, we know from
Equation

3
that
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell
lands in the automorphism group of this canonical triple, and so we have a commutative diagram

(7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Spin}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{O}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f),\underline{\sigma},\underline{f}).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PGO}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}_{\Inn}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbf{O}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.90417pt\ell}

There are two final groups we will need. For this, we assume
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
is a quadratic triple of even rank. In this case,
[
CF15
, 4.2.0.15]
says that the center of
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
, denoted
\mathcal{Z}
, is a finite étale
\mathcal{O}
–algebra of degree
2
. Since any automorphism of an algebra restricts to an automorphism of the center, this gives us a map

\mathrm{Arf}\colon\mathbf{O}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f),\underline{\sigma})\to\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}

called the
Arf map
in
[
CF15
, 4.3.0.27]
(and over rings is called the
Dickson map
in
[
Knu91
, IV.5.1]
). Of course, there is also another Arf map

\mathrm{Arf}^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{PGO}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.90417pt\ell}\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f),\underline{\sigma})\to\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}

and
\mathrm{Arf}=\mathrm{Arf^{\prime}}\circ\pi_{\mathbf{O}}
. We set

\displaystyle\mathbf{O}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}^{+}

\displaystyle=\Ker(\mathrm{Arf})\text{, and}

\displaystyle\mathbf{PGO}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}^{+}

\displaystyle=\Ker(\mathrm{Arf}^{\prime}).

When
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
is adjoint to
(\mathcal{M},q)
we denote these
\mathbf{O}_{q}^{+}
and
\mathbf{PGO}_{q}^{+}
. When
(\mathcal{M},q)=(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),q_{2n})
is the even rank hyperbolic quadratic form of
Example

1.1
, we write
\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}
and
\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}
. The vector representation factors through
\mathbf{O}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}^{+}
, in particular we have surjective maps of sheaves

\mathbf{Spin}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}\overset{\chi}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathbf{O}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}^{+}\overset{\pi_{\mathbf{O}}}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathbf{PGO}_{(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)}^{+}.

These groups are semisimple groups of type
D
. The spin groups are simply connected and
\mathbf{PGO}^{+}
is adjoint.

A small fact we mention here for later use is that the vector representation respects isomorphisms of quadratic triples.

Lemma 1.3
.

Let
\psi\colon(\mathcal{A}_{1},\sigma_{1},f_{1})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(\mathcal{A}_{2},\sigma_{2},f_{2})
be an isomorphism of quadratic triples. Then, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Spin}_{(\mathcal{A}_{1},\sigma_{1},f_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Spin}_{(\mathcal{A}_{2},\sigma_{2},f_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{O}^{+}_{(\mathcal{A}_{1},\sigma_{1},f_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{O}^{+}_{(\mathcal{A}_{2},\sigma_{2},f_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.90417pt\ell(\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

commutes where
\chi_{i}
are the respective vector representations.

Proof.

It is clear from the definitions of
\mathbf{Spin}
and
\mathbf{O}^{+}
that the algebra automorphisms
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\psi)
and
\psi
restrict to group homomorphisms as in the diagram. Since we may check the commutativity of the diagram over a cover, by working sufficiently locally we may assume that
\mathcal{A}_{i}=\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M}_{i})
and that
(\sigma_{i},f_{i})
is adjoint to a regular quadratic form
q_{i}\colon\mathcal{M}_{i}\to\mathcal{O}
for each
i
. Furthermore, we may assume that
\psi
is induced by an isometry
\phi\colon(\mathcal{M}_{1},q_{1})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(\mathcal{M}_{2},q_{2})
, i.e., that

\displaystyle\psi\colon\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M}_{1})

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{M}_{2})

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\mapsto\phi\circ B\circ\phi^{-1}.

Additionally, we will have
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{A}_{1},\sigma_{1},f_{1})\cong\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{1},q_{1})
and
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\psi)(m_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes m_{k})=\phi(m_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes\phi(m_{k})
.

Now, consider
a\in\mathbf{Spin}_{(\mathcal{A}_{1},\sigma_{1},f_{1})}
. The element
\psi(\chi_{1}(a))
is the section of
\mathbf{O}^{+}_{(\mathcal{A}_{2},\sigma_{2},f_{2})}
which behaves as

\displaystyle\psi(\chi_{1}(a))(n)

\displaystyle=(\phi\circ\chi_{1}(a)\circ\phi^{-1})(n)

\displaystyle=\phi\big(a\cdot\phi^{-1}(n)\cdot a^{-1}\big)

where
a\cdot\phi^{-1}(n)\cdot a^{-1}
lies in
\mathcal{M}_{1}\subset\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{M}_{1},q_{1})
. By definition, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{M}_{1},q_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{M}_{2},q_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.90417pt\ell(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

commutes and
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\phi)|_{\mathcal{C}\hskip-0.90417pt\ell_{0}(\mathcal{M}_{1},q_{1})}=\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\psi)
. So, identifying
\mathcal{M}_{1}
with its image in
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathcal{M}_{1},q_{1})
we have

\displaystyle\phi\big(a\cdot\phi^{-1}(n)\cdot a^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\phi)\big(a\cdot\phi^{-1}(n)\cdot a^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\psi)(a)\cdot\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\phi)(\phi^{-1}(n))\cdot\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\phi)(a)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\psi)(a)\cdot n\cdot\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\psi)(a)^{-1}.

Therefore, we see that
\psi(\chi_{1}(a))=\chi_{2}(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\psi)(a))
, which proves the claim.
∎

1.C.ii.
Chevalley Generators

For the rest of this section we work with
(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),q_{2n})
of
Example

1.1
. We fix the ordered basis

\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n},v_{n}^{*},\ldots,v_{2}^{*},v_{1}^{*}\}

of
\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V})
and use it to identify
\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}))=\Mat_{2n}(\mathcal{O})
.

The groups
\mathbf{Spin}_{2n}
,
\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}
, and
\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}
are Chevalley groups. In particular, they have elementary subpresheaves of groups generated by Chevalley generators. For background on Chevalley groups over fields and algebraic relations between Chevalley generators we refer to
[
Ste68
]
.

All three of these groups are of type
D_{n}=\{\pm e_{i}\pm e_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq n\}
. We recall from
[
Rue21
, 1.3.21]
that we may choose the following elements as Chevalley generators of
\mathbf{Spin}_{2n}\subseteq\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.29167pt\ell(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V}),q_{2n})
where
1\leq i<j\leq n
.

\displaystyle X_{e_{i}-e_{j}}(t)

\displaystyle=1+tv_{i}v_{j}^{*}

\displaystyle X_{-e_{i}+e_{j}}(t)

\displaystyle=1+tv_{j}v_{i}^{*}

\displaystyle X_{e_{i}+e_{j}}(t)

\displaystyle=1+tv_{i}v_{j}

\displaystyle X_{-e_{i}-e_{j}}(t)

\displaystyle=1+tv_{j}^{*}v_{i}^{*}

By
[
Rue21
, 1.3.22]
(where
\mathbf{O}^{+}
is called
\mathbf{SO}
), these map via
\chi
to the following choice of Chevalley generators for
\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}
.

\displaystyle x_{e_{i}-e_{j}}(t)

\displaystyle=I+t(E_{ij}-E_{\bar{j}\bar{i}})

\displaystyle x_{-e_{i}+e_{j}}(t)

\displaystyle=I+t(E_{ji}-E_{\bar{i}\bar{j}})

\displaystyle x_{e_{i}+e_{j}}(t)

\displaystyle=I+t(E_{i\bar{j}}-E_{j\bar{i}})

\displaystyle x_{-e_{i}-e_{j}}(t)

\displaystyle=I+t(E_{\bar{j}i}-E_{\bar{i}j})

where
\bar{i}=2n+1-i
and
E_{ij}
is the matrix with
1
in the
(i,j)
entry and zeroes elsewhere. We choose the Chevalley generators

x^{\prime}_{\alpha}(t)=\pi_{\mathbf{O}}(x_{\alpha}(t))

for
\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}
. These choices then define presheaves of subgroups called the
elementary subgroups
.

Definition 1.4
.

We define the following functors into groups.

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{Spin}}\colon\Sch_{S}

\displaystyle\to\Grp

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle X_{\alpha}(t)\mid\alpha\in D_{n},\;t\in\mathcal{O}(T)\rangle

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{O}^{+}}\colon\Sch_{S}

\displaystyle\to\Grp

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle x_{\alpha}(t)\mid\alpha\in D_{n},\;t\in\mathcal{O}(T)\rangle

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{PGO}^{+}}\colon\Sch_{S}

\displaystyle\to\Grp

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\langle x^{\prime}_{\alpha}(t)\mid\alpha\in D_{n},\;t\in\mathcal{O}(T)\rangle.

Proposition 1.5
.

The sheafification of
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{Spin}}
is
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{Spin}}^{\sharp}=\mathbf{Spin}_{2n}
. Similarly,
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{O}^{+}}^{\sharp}=\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}
and
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{PGO}^{+}}^{\sharp}=\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to argue that
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{O}^{+}}^{\sharp}=\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}
. If this is true, then the image of this group under the homomorphism
\pi_{\mathbf{O}}
will be both
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{PGO}^{+}}^{\sharp}
and
\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}
, thereby implying the last claim. For the first claim, using
[
Ste68
, Lemma 28(d)]
(which is only stated over fields but holds over any ring), one can compute that the center of
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{Spin}}
contains the elements

h_{e_{n-1}-e_{n}}(t)h_{e_{n-1}+e_{n}}(t)

for
t\in\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
where the elements
h_{\alpha}(t)
are as defined in
[
Ste68
, Lemma 19]
. These elements also form the kernel of
\chi\colon\mathbf{Spin}_{2n}\to\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}
, and therefore
\Ker(\chi)\subseteq\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{Spin}}
. Thus, for every section
a\in\mathbf{Spin}_{2n}
, since
\chi(a)
locally belongs to
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{O}^{+}}
by assumption and
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{Spin}}(T)\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{O}^{+}}(T)
is surjective for all
T\in\Sch_{S}
, the section
a
will locally belong to
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{Spin}}
. Hence
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{Spin}}^{\sharp}=\mathbf{Spin}_{2n}
, verifying the last claim.

Hence, we now argue that
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{O}^{+}}^{\sharp}=\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}
. The argument hinges on the following fact about the fppf topology. For every ring
R
and element
r\in R
, the ring extension
R\to R[x]/\langle x^{2}-r\rangle
is finitely presented and is also faithfully flat since
R[x]/\langle x^{2}-r\rangle\cong R\oplus Rx
is free as an
R
–module. Therefore,
\{\Spec(R[x]/\langle x^{2}-r\rangle)\to\Spec(R)\}
is an fppf covering. This means that when considering an fppf covering of
S
by affine schemes, we may assume that any element of
R
has a square root in
R
by refining the cover if necessary.

Now, let
T\in\Sch_{S}
and
A\in\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}(T)
. Let
\{U_{i}\to T\}_{i\in I}
be an affine open cover. Then
A|_{U_{i}}\in\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}(U_{i})\subset\Mat_{2n}(\mathcal{O}(U_{i}))
is a matrix with entries
A|_{U_{i}}=[a_{ij}]
. Since
A|_{U_{i}}
is invertible,
\det(A|_{U_{i}})\in\mathcal{O}(U_{i})^{\times}
, which means
\langle a_{11},a_{12},\ldots,a_{1n}\rangle=\mathcal{O}(U_{i})
. So, by refining the open cover to include the localizations
\Spec(\mathcal{O}(U_{i})_{a_{1j}})
if needed, we may assume at least one of the entries in the first row of
A|_{U_{i}}
is invertible. Furthermore, since
(q_{2n}\circ(A|_{U_{i}})^{-1})(v_{n}^{*})=q_{2n}(v_{n}^{*})=0
and
(A|_{U_{i}})^{-1}=\sigma(A|_{U_{i}})
which has final column
\begin{bmatrix}a_{1,2n}&a_{1,2n-1}&\ldots&a_{11}\end{bmatrix}^{T}
, we obtain the relation that

\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{1,k}a_{1,2n+1-k}=0.

With these facts, and the square root of elements as necessary, we can repeat the calculations for
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{SO}_{2n}}
from the proof of
[
Rue21
, 3.4.8]
to show that
A|_{U_{i}}\in\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{O}^{+}}(U_{i})
. Since
A
is locally in
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{O}^{+}}
we know that
A\in\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{O}^{+}}^{\sharp}(T)
, and so
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{O}^{+}}^{\sharp}=\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}
as desired.
∎

Remark 1.6
.

The above result is also an application of
[
SGA3
, Expose XXIII, 3.5.4]
, which proves the claim more generally for groups of any type with a pinning. In their setup, one has unipotent root subgroups as well as a torus which generate the subsheaf together. One can show that the elements
h_{\alpha}(t)
generate an appropriate torus and therefore the result from
[
SGA3
]
applies.

Furthermore, the above result is stated for, and the proof inherently uses, the fppf topology. However, in some cases weaker topologies are sufficient. It was known by E. Abe in
[
Abe69
]
and H. Matsumoto in
[
Mat69
]
that
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{G}}(R)=\mathbf{G}(R)
when
\mathbf{G}
is simply connected and
R
is a local ring. This implies that for simply connected groups,
\mathbf{G}
is the Zariski sheafification of
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{G}}
. In particular this holds for
\mathbf{Spin}_{2n}
. Alternatively, if we assume that
\frac{1}{2}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
, then whenever we need to add a square root of an element in the above argument, we may do so with an étale cover. Therefore in this case,
\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}
and
\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}
are the étale sheafifications of their elementary subgroups.

Proposition

1.5
will allow us to use calculations on Chevalley generators to identify homomorphisms between these groups, since a map of presheaves between the elementary subgroups extends uniquely to a morphism between the sheaves of groups.

1.D.
Cohomology, Stacks, and Twisting

We will make use of first non-abelian cohomology in the style of
[
Gir
, 2.4.2]
. For a sheaf of groups
\mathbf{G}\colon\Sch_{S}\to\Grp
, a
\mathbf{G}
–torsor
is a sheaf of sets
\mathcal{P}\colon\Sch_{S}\to\Grp
with a right
\mathbf{G}
–action, i.e., a natural transformation
\mathcal{P}\times\mathbf{G}\to\mathcal{P}
such that

(i)

for all
T\in\Sch_{S}
, the map
\mathcal{P}(T)\times\mathbf{G}(T)\to\mathcal{P}(T)
defines a simply transitive right
\mathbf{G}(T)
–action on
\mathcal{P}(T)
, and

(ii)

there exists an fppf cover
\{T_{i}\to S\}_{i\in I}
over which
\mathcal{P}(T_{i})\neq\O
for all
i\in I
.

A torsor
\mathcal{P}
may have
\mathcal{P}(T)=\O
for some
T\in\Sch_{S}
. In this case we view the map
\O\times\mathbf{G}(T)\to\O
as vacuously giving a simply transitive action of
\mathbf{G}(T)
on
\O
. A morphism of
\mathbf{G}
–torsors is a natural transformation of sheaves which is equivariant with respect to the right
\mathbf{G}
–actions. Any morphism of torsors must be an isomorphism. The sheaf of sets
\mathbf{G}
itself viewed as torsor via right multiplication is called the
trivial torsor
. The first cohomology set
H^{1}(S,\mathbf{G})
is defined to be the pointed set of isomorphism classes of
\mathbf{G}
–torsors with the class of the trivial torsor as the base point.

Two sheaves
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
on
\Sch_{S}
are called
twisted forms
of each other if they are locally isomorphic, i.e., if there exists a cover
\{T_{i}\to S\}_{i\in I}
over which
\mathcal{F}|_{T_{i}}\cong\mathcal{G}|_{T_{i}}
for all
i\in I
. In this case, the sheaf of internal isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{s\hskip-0.6458pto\hskip-0.6458ptm}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\colon\Sch_{S}

\displaystyle\to\Sets

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\Isom(\mathcal{F}|_{T},\mathcal{G}|_{T})

is an
\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{F})
–torsor. For example, if
(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f)
is a quadratic triple of constant rank
2n
, then the sheaf of quadratic triple isomorphisms

\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{s\hskip-0.6458pto\hskip-0.6458ptm}((\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{V})),\sigma_{q_{2n}},f_{q_{2n}}),(\mathcal{A},\sigma,f))

is a
\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}
–torsor.

We will also make use of stacks over
\Sch_{S}
following
[
Ols
]
and
[
Vis
]
. Let
p\colon\mathfrak{F}\to\Sch_{S}
be a stack. For
T\in\Sch_{S}
we denote by
\mathfrak{F}(T)
the fiber of
\mathfrak{F}
over
T
. For a morphism
f\colon T^{\prime}\to T
of
S
–schemes and an object
x\in\mathfrak{F}(T)
, a pullback of
x
will be denoted
f^{*}(x)\in\mathfrak{F}(T^{\prime})
or simply by
x|_{T^{\prime}}
when
f
is clear. The automorphism sheaf of an object
x\in\mathfrak{F}(T)
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{Aut}(x)\colon\Sch_{T}

\displaystyle\to\Grp

\displaystyle T^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto\Aut_{\mathfrak{F}(T^{\prime})}(x|_{T^{\prime}}).

Two objects
x,y\in\mathfrak{F}(T)
in the same fiber of a stack are called
twisted forms
if they are locally isomorphic, i.e., there is a cover
\{T_{i}\to T\}_{i\in I}
such that
x|_{T_{i}}\cong y|_{T_{i}}
in
\mathfrak{F}(T_{i})
for all
i\in I
. In this case,
\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{s\hskip-0.6458pto\hskip-0.6458ptm}(x,y)
is an
\mathbf{Aut}(x)
–torsor over
T
.

When the stack
\mathfrak{F}
is a gerbe with
x\in\mathfrak{F}(S)
, the isomorphism classes of
\mathfrak{F}(S)
are classified by
H^{1}(S,\mathbf{Aut}(x))
. Following
[
Ols
, 3.1.10]
, a morphism
\varphi\colon\mathfrak{F}\to\mathfrak{G}
of stacks (or more generally fibered categories) is called an
equivalence
if for every
T\in\Sch_{S}
the functor between fibers
\varphi_{T}\colon\mathfrak{F}(T)\to\mathfrak{G}(T)
is an equivalence of categories.

Lemma 1.7

(
[
Gir
]
,
[
GNR24
, 1.19]
)
.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathfrak{F}\to\mathfrak{G}
be a morphism of gerbes. Assume there is an object
x\in\mathfrak{F}(S)
. If the associated group homomorphism

\mathbf{Aut}(\varphi)\colon\mathbf{Aut}(x)\to\mathbf{Aut}(\varphi(x))

is an isomorphism, then
\varphi
is an equivalence of stacks.

We attribute the above lemma to two sources because it is a folklore result which easily follows from results in
[
Gir
]
, but does not appear there in this form. A proof which assembles the relevant facts from
[
Gir
]
is given in
[
GNR24
, 1.19]
.

One motivation for our use of stacks is to take advantage of the following result.

Lemma 1.8

(
[
Gir
, 2.5.3(i)]
)
.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathfrak{F}\to\mathfrak{G}
be a morphism of gerbes. For an object
x\in\mathfrak{F}(S)
, the associated group homomorphism
\mathbf{Aut}(\varphi)\colon\mathbf{Aut}(x)\to\mathbf{Aut}(\varphi(x))
induces the following map on cohomology

\displaystyle H^{1}(S,\mathbf{Aut}(x))

\displaystyle\to H^{1}(S,\mathbf{Aut}(\varphi(x)))

\displaystyle[x^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\mapsto[\varphi(x^{\prime})]

where
[x^{\prime}]
denotes the isomorphism class of
x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{F}(S)
and
[\varphi(x^{\prime})]
the isomorphism class of
\varphi(x^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{G}(S)
.

1.E.
Étale Covers

By definition, an
étale cover of degree
d
of a scheme
T
is an affine morphism
f\colon L\to T
such that
f_{*}(\mathcal{O}|_{L})
is an étale
\mathcal{O}|_{T}
–algebra of rank
d
, i.e., it is a twisted form as commutative
\mathcal{O}|_{T}
–algebras of
\mathcal{O}|_{T}^{d}
. As in
[
CF15
, 2.5.2.9]
, since
\mathbf{Aut}(\mathcal{O}|_{T}^{d})\cong\mathbb{S}_{d}|_{T}
is the constant group sheaf associated to the permutation group of
d
letters, isomorphism classes of degree
d
étale covers of
T
are classified by
H^{1}(T,\mathbb{S}_{d})
. If
f\colon L\to T
is such a cover, since
f_{*}(\mathcal{O}|_{T})
is locally isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}|_{T}^{d}
, there will be a cover
\{T_{i}\to T\}_{i\in I}
such that
L\times_{T}T_{i}\cong T_{i}^{\sqcup d}
. This provides an equivalent characterization of étale covers of degree
d
.

To set our conventions,
\mathbf{Aut}_{S}(S^{\sqcup d})\cong\mathbb{S}_{d}
acts by permuting components as follows. Let
T\in\Sch_{S}
and write
T=\sqcup_{i\in I}T_{i}
as the disjoint union of its connected components. Writing
P_{d}
for the abstract permutation group of
\{1,\ldots,d\}
, we thus have
\mathbb{S}_{d}(T)\cong\prod_{i\in I}P_{d}
and we write
\beta=(\beta_{i})_{i\in I}
for a section over
T
. Now,

T^{d}=\big(\bigsqcup_{i\in I}T_{i,1}\big)\sqcup\ldots\sqcup\big(\bigsqcup_{i\in I}T_{i,d}\big)

where
T_{i,1}=T_{i,2}=\ldots=T_{i,d}=T_{i}
for all
i\in I
. Then,

(8)

\displaystyle\beta\colon T^{d}

\displaystyle\to T^{d}

\displaystyle T_{i,k}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\Id}T_{i,\beta_{i}^{-1}(k)},

by which we mean,
\beta
sends the
T_{i,k}
component to the
T_{i,\beta_{i}^{-1}(k)}
component via the identity. Alternatively,
T_{i,\beta_{i}(k)}
ends up in the
T_{i,k}
position. However, since
\mathbb{S}_{d}
is the sheafification of the constant presheaf
\mathbb{P}_{d}
associated to the abstract group
P_{d}
, we will often be able to assume without loss of generality that
\beta\in\mathbb{S}_{3}(T)
is a
diagonal section
, i.e., that
\beta_{i}=\beta_{j}
for all
i,j\in I
or equivalently that
\beta\in\mathbb{P}_{3}(T)\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{3}(T)
. For example, showing that a homomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathbb{S}_{3}\to\mathbb{S}_{3}
restricts to an isomorphism
\mathbb{P}_{3}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{P}_{3}
is sufficient to conclude that
\varphi
itself is an isomorphism.

Analogously to what is done over fields in
[
KT03
, §2.1]
, we will use the construction of
\Sigma_{2}(L)
from a degree
3
étale cover
L\to S
. We review this construction explicitly in this case. Writing the set of size six as
\{(1,2),(2,3),(3,1),(1,3),(2,1),(3,2)\}
induces a group homomorphisms
P_{3}\hookrightarrow P_{6}
by allowing elements of
P_{3}
to act componentwise on the pairs. This homomorphism of abstract groups then corresponds to a homomorphism
\mathbb{S}_{3}\to\mathbb{S}_{6}
of the associated constant group sheaves. Now, by the above discussion, a degree
3
étale cover
f\colon L\to S
corresponds to an
\mathbb{S}_{3}
–torsor
\mathcal{P}_{L}
over
S
. Therefore,
\mathcal{P}_{L}\wedge^{\mathbb{S}_{3}}\mathbb{S}_{6}
is an
\mathbb{S}_{6}
–torsor which corresponds to a degree
6
étale cover which we denote by
f^{\prime}\colon\Sigma_{2}(L)\to S
.

Now consider the split degree
6
étale cover,

(9)

S^{\sqcup 6}=(S_{1,2}\sqcup S_{2,3}\sqcup S_{3,1})\sqcup(S_{1,3}\sqcup S_{2,1}\sqcup S_{3,2})

where each
S_{i,j}=S
also. We then have two degree
2
étale covers

\displaystyle\pi\colon S^{\sqcup 6}

\displaystyle\to S^{\sqcup 3}

\displaystyle\tau\colon S^{\sqcup 6}

\displaystyle\to S^{\sqcup 3}

\displaystyle x\in S_{i,j}

\displaystyle\mapsto x\in S_{i}

\displaystyle x\in S_{i,j}

\displaystyle\mapsto x\in S_{j}

by which we mean,
\pi
sends the
i,j
labelled component of
S^{\sqcup 6}
to the
i
labelled component of
S^{\sqcup 3}
via the identity map
S\to S
, and likewise
\tau
sends the
i,j
component to the
j
component. These maps correspond to the two morphisms of étale
\mathcal{O}
–algebras

\displaystyle\pi^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{O}^{3}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{O}^{6}

\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{O}^{3}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{O}^{6}

\displaystyle(a,b,c)

\displaystyle\mapsto(a,b,c,a,b,c)

\displaystyle(a,b,c)

\displaystyle\mapsto(b,c,a,c,a,b).

It is straightforward to check that both of these maps are equivariant with respect to the
\mathbb{S}_{3}
action on
\mathcal{O}^{3}
and on
\mathcal{O}^{6}
via the homomorphism
\mathbb{S}_{3}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}_{6}
, and therefore they twist into morphisms
\pi_{L}^{\prime},\tau_{L}^{\prime}\colon f_{*}(\mathcal{O}|_{L})\to f^{\prime}_{*}(\mathcal{O}|_{\Sigma_{2}(L)})
which correspond to two degree
2
étale covers

\pi_{L}\colon\Sigma_{2}(L)\to L\text{ and }\tau_{L}\colon\Sigma_{2}(L)\to L.

We call
\Sigma_{2}(L)
the
trialitarian cover
of
L
.

Lemma 1.9
.

Let
L\to S
be a degree
3
étale cover. For any scheme
T\in\Sch_{S}
, the pullback
L\times_{S}T\to T
is another degree
3
étale cover. In this case, we have an isomorphism of schemes
\Sigma_{2}(L)\times_{S}T\cong\Sigma_{2}(L\times_{S}T)
.

Proof.

This can be seen in terms of cohomology. There is a restriction map
H^{1}(S,\mathbb{S}_{3})\to H^{1}(T,\mathbb{S}_{3}|_{T})
which sends the class of a torsor
[\mathcal{P}]
to the class
[\mathcal{P}|_{T}]
. Likewise, there is a restriction map for
\mathbb{S}_{6}
. In addition, we have maps
H^{1}(S,\mathbb{S}_{3})\to H^{1}(S,\mathbb{S}_{6})
and
H^{1}(T,\mathbb{S}_{3}|_{T})\to H^{1}(T,\mathbb{S}_{6}|_{T})
coming from our group homomorphism
\mathbb{S}_{3}\to\mathbb{S}_{6}
. These fit into a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(S,\mathbb{S}_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(T,\mathbb{S}_{3}|_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(S,\mathbb{S}_{6})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(T,\mathbb{S}_{6}|_{T}).}

Tracing the class of the cover
L\to S
through this diagram, we obtain

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[L]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[L\times_{S}T]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\Sigma_{2}(L)]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\Sigma_{2}(L)\times_{S}T]}={[\Sigma_{2}(L\times_{S}T)]}}

which justifies our claim.
∎

We construct one additional map, an isomorphism of
\Sigma_{2}(L)
, which will be used to define trialitarian algebras. Consider the isomorphism of
\rho\colon S^{\sqcup 6}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}S^{\sqcup 6}
given by the permutation

(10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S_{1,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2,3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{3,1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S_{1,3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{3,2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S_{1,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2,3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{3,1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S_{1,3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqcup}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{3,2})}

where the arrows are the identity
S\to S
. Viewed as an element in
\mathbb{S}_{6}(S)
, this is
(1\;3\;2)(4\;5\;6)
according to our conventions in
Equation

8
. Given an
\mathcal{O}|_{S^{\sqcup 6}}
–algebra
\mathcal{E}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{3},\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2},\mathcal{B}_{3})
, the pullback along
\rho
is

(11)

\rho^{*}(\mathcal{E})=(\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{3},\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{3},\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}).

The scheme isomorphism
\rho
corresponds to the
\mathcal{O}
–algebra isomorphism

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{O}^{6}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{O}^{6}

\displaystyle((a,b,c),(d,e,f))

\displaystyle\mapsto((b,c,a),(f,d,e)).

Lemma 1.10
.

The isomorphism
\rho^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{S}_{3}
–equivariant.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, it is sufficient to check this for the diagonal permutations
c=(1\,2\,3)
and
\lambda=(2\,3)
of
\mathbb{S}_{3}(T)
.

Since
\rho^{\prime}
is of the form
(c,c^{-1})
, it is clear it commutes with the action of
c
, which is of the form
(c,c)
.

The action of
\lambda
performs the permutation

((a,b,c),(d,e,f))\mapsto((d,f,e),(a,c,b)).

Therefore we can compute that

(\rho^{\prime}\circ\lambda)(a,b,c),(d,e,f))=\rho^{\prime}((d,f,e),(a,c,b))=((f,e,d),(b,a,c))

and that

(\lambda\circ\rho^{\prime})((a,b,c),(d,e,f))=\lambda((b,c,a),(f,d,e))=((f,e,d),(b,a,c))

and see that
\rho^{\prime}
commutes with
\lambda
as desired.
∎

Therefore, this map descends to an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}
–algebras

\rho_{\Sigma_{2}(L)}^{\prime}\colon f^{\prime}_{*}(\mathcal{O}|_{\Sigma_{2}(L)})\to f^{\prime}_{*}(\mathcal{O}|_{\Sigma_{2}(L)})

which corresponds to an isomorphism of schemes
\rho_{\Sigma_{2}(L)}\colon\Sigma_{2}(L)\to\Sigma_{2}(L)
. It is order
3
since
\rho
is.

2.
Triality Without Octonions

In this section we demonstrate that phenomena surrounding triality which are described in
[
KMRT
, Ch. X]
also exist in our context over a scheme. We do so using computations with Chevalley generators in place of the traditional computations with Octonions.

2.A.
Automorphism Groups of Groups

We fix a
pinning
of each of the groups
\mathbf{Spin}_{8}
,
\mathbf{O}_{8}^{+}
, and
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}
in the style of
[
SGA3
, XXIII.1.1]
. This first involves a choice of maximal torus for each group. Using the theory of Chevalley generators, if
x_{\alpha}(t)
are generators for a split group
\mathbf{G}
, then setting

w_{\alpha}(t)=x_{\alpha}(t)x_{-\alpha}(-t^{-1})x_{\alpha}(t)\text{ and }h_{\alpha}(t)=w_{\alpha}(t)w_{\alpha}(-1)

for
t\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}
produces a commutative diagonalizable subgroup presheaf generated by sections
h_{\alpha}(t)
. We let
\mathbf{T}
be the sheafification of this presheaf, which is a maximal torus of
\mathbf{G}
. In
\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}
with our choice of generators as in
Section

1.C.ii
, this takes the familiar form of diagonal elements

\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}}=\{\diag(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n},t_{n}^{-1},\ldots,t_{1}^{-1})\in\Mat_{2n}(\mathcal{O})\mid t_{i}\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}\}.

For the simply connected group
\mathbf{Spin}_{2n}
, the character lattice
\Hom(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{Spin}_{2n}},\mathbb{G}_{m})=\Lambda_{w}
is the weight lattice of the root system
D_{n}
, while the adjoint group’s character lattice
\Hom(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}},\mathbb{G}_{m})=\Lambda_{r}
is the root lattice. These lattices are mutual duals and are isomorphic. The intermediate group
\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}
has the intermediate character lattice
\Lambda=\Hom(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{O}_{2n}^{+}},\mathbb{G}_{m})=\Span_{\mathbb{Z}}(\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\})
. The data of a simple system
\Delta\subseteq D_{n}
, these character lattices, and our fixed choices of Chevalley generators defines a pinning for each of these three groups.

Given a graph automorphism of the Dynkin diagram of
D_{n}
relative to the simple system
\Delta
, it corresponds to a bijection
\varphi\colon\Delta\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Delta
which extends to an automorphism of the root system and hence also to an automorphism of the root and weight lattices. If this automorphism stabilizes the associated character lattice (which is automatic when
\mathbf{G}
is simply connected or adjoint) then it extends uniquely to an automorphism of the elementary subgroup
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{G}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{G}}
defined by sending the Chevalley generators
x_{\alpha}(t)\mapsto x_{\varphi(\alpha)}(t)
and
x_{-\alpha}(t)\mapsto x_{-\varphi(\alpha)}(t)
for all
\alpha\in\Delta
. The images of the
x_{\alpha}(t)
for other roots
\alpha\notin\Delta
are then determined by the commutator relations of the group. By
Proposition

1.5
, this isomorphism extends to a unique isomorphism of
\mathbf{G}
, which we also denote
\varphi\colon\mathbf{G}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{G}
.

Let
\Lambda
be the character lattice of
\mathbf{G}
and let
\mathbf{Aut}(\Delta,\Lambda)
denote the constant group sheaf associated to the abstract group
\Aut(\Delta,\Lambda)
of graph automorphisms stabilizing
\Lambda
. By the discussion above, we have a group homomorphisms
a\colon\mathbf{Aut}(\Delta,\Lambda)\to\mathbf{Aut}(\mathbf{G})
. For any of the three groups we are considering, the canonical morphism
\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}
has a central kernel and the center of
\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}
is trivial, so the group of inner automorphisms of
\mathbf{G}
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}
. This gives a morphism
\Inn\colon\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}\to\mathbf{Aut}(\mathbf{G})
. Then, by
[
SGA3
, XXIV.1.3]
we have an exact sequence

1\to\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}\xrightarrow{\Inn}\mathbf{Aut}(\mathbf{G})\xrightarrow{p}\Out(\mathbf{G})\to 1

where
\Out(\mathbf{G})=\mathbf{Aut}(\mathbf{G})/\Inn(\mathbf{G})
is the quotient sheaf of automorphisms modulo inner automorphisms. Furthermore,
[
SGA3
, XXIV.1.3(iii)]
states that
p\circ a\colon\mathbf{Aut}(\Delta,\Lambda)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Out(\mathbf{G})
is an isomorphism. Finally,
[
SGA3
, XXIV.1.4]
tells us that the automorphism group of
\mathbf{G}
is the semidirect product

\mathbf{Aut}(\mathbf{G})=\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}\rtimes\mathbf{Aut}(\Delta,\Lambda).

where
\mathbf{Aut}(\Delta,\Lambda)
acts on
\mathbf{PGO}_{2n}^{+}
by acting on Chevalley generators as described above since any graph automorphism fixes the root lattice.

Focusing on
\mathbf{Spin}_{8}
,
\mathbf{O}_{8}^{+}
, and
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}
, we work with the following non-standard simple system
\Delta
of the root system
D_{4}
.

e_{3}-e_{4}
e_{1}-e_{3}
-e_{1}-e_{3}
e_{2}+e_{4}

The justification for this choice is explained in
Remark

2.5
. If
\mathbf{G}
is one of
\mathbf{Spin}_{8}
or
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}
, then the associated lattice is either the weight or root lattice, and so
\mathbf{Aut}(\Delta,\Lambda)=\mathbf{Aut}(\Delta)=\mathbb{S}_{3}
is the constant group sheaf of permutations of
3
elements, viewed as the graph isomorphisms of the simple system above. Therefore,

(12)

\mathbf{Aut}(\mathbf{G})=\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}\rtimes\mathbb{S}_{3}.

We denote by
\theta^{+}
the order
3
automorphism of
\Delta
coming from a counter-clockwise turn of the graph, and let
\theta^{-}=(\theta^{+})^{2}
be its inverse.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be one of
\mathbf{Spin}_{8}
or
\mathbf{PGO}_{8}^{+}
and let
x_{\alpha}(t)
denote its Chevalley generators as chosen in
Section

1.C.ii
. The automorphism
\theta^{+}\colon\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
behaves as follows on Chevalley generators.

\displaystyle x_{e_{1}-e_{2}}(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{e_{3}+e_{4}}(-t)

\displaystyle x_{-e_{1}+e_{2}}(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{-e_{3}-e_{4}}(-t)

\displaystyle x_{e_{1}-e_{3}}(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{e_{2}+e_{4}}(t)

\displaystyle x_{-e_{1}+e_{3}}(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{-e_{2}-e_{4}}(t)
